Bellatrix, he knew, a bright spark of anger flaring in his chest at the name, but instead of landing in the atrium, with its black stone and green fire and that woman on the ground before him, he stumbled into the Gryffindor common room on too-short legs.

“What…?” Harry said, stepping back and shoving some of his hair from his face. He turned in a slow circle, and the flood of confusion and hurt that had been driving him forward dropped away under the strength of his frustration when he looked down at his stick-thin arms. “What the hell is this?” he hissed, glaring at the crackling hearth.

Was it Death? Was It doing this to him, dragging him from memory to memory to just rub it all in? To make him face all the things, the people and places, that he would never get to have again? Or was this just his own demons tormenting him?

Harry pressed his hands into his mouth, letting out a tumultuous breath. He could feel his chin begin to shake, a rush of heat crawling up his cheeks and towards his eyes, and he slammed them shut in denial. “Not now,” he said, voice reedy, “not now.”

He jabbed his tongue into his cheek hard to starve it off just as the voices started up again.

Hermione, Ron and Neville this time, high with youth but still recognisable. He turned his back on them immediately, because if he saw them like this – young and vulnerable and so unprepared for the life ahead of them – he would break.

“There’s got to be a way out of this,” Harry muttered as the eleven-year-old versions of his friends squabbled in the background.

He moved to the opening that would take him to the dormitories, the sound of Neville’s petrified form dropping to the ground and the deep thud of something knocking into the Fat Lady’s portrait chasing after him. Harry raced up the stairs on his skinny legs, taking them two at a time, until he reached the first-year boy’s dormitory.

The overwhelming sensation of a presence coming up behind him sent him through this door, slamming it closed behind him.

Harry leaned against the wood, hands pressed flat to it, hoping that this one would be enough to keep whatever that thing was out.

He just wanted a second to stop, to not be hunted.

A second of peace.

Harry knocked his forehead on the door. “Shit,” he cursed, because it was that or drop to his knees and cry.

“Language,” someone chided with a tinkling laugh.

Harry spun around with a harsh inhale and recoiled when he saw where he had been brought now.

A poorly rendered impression of his apartment’s living room was forming around him, growing more vibrant and accurate as the seconds ticked on – including the lounge and the woman sitting on it, her arm braced along the back, chin resting on her forearm.

His eyes widened, lips parting in wonder.

“Ginny,” he breathed rapturously.

Ginny smiled at him, silent laughter lighting up her features the longer he stared, until the colour of her eyes resembled liquid caramel – warm and absolutely lovely. With her hair bundled messily atop her head, she was the most beautiful thing he had ever seen.

“Hey Harry,” she said, and just hearing her say his name had the tears he had been fighting pushing forward. His face was hot and itchy, as if the skin was pulled too tight over his cheeks.

The need to touch her came at him with the force of a hurricane, powerful enough to leave him quivering, and he wrapped his arms around himself to try and quell it. His knuckles turned white.

His eyes darted to the banner hanging up behind her, the one he had bought to celebrate her official acceptance onto the Holyhead Harpies. “This isn’t real,” he told himself, shaking his head. “This is just another memory. You’re not her.”

The disappointment was suffocating.

She tipped her head to the side, smile dimming but not disappearing, and compared to Ron and Hermione her eyes were damningly aware.

“This is all in my head,” he continued, voice firming.

“You know that doesn’t mean it’s not real,” Ginny replied quietly.

Harry stopped, his shoulders tensing, because he was sure that Ginny had never said those words to him.

He fixed his stare down between his boots, trying to strangle the unnamed emotion welling up inside him. But the pull was too much, and he inevitably looked at her, drinking her in.

Ginny stared back at him, and the fact that she did not say more, that she was not saying anything related to quidditch or her training schedule or the travelling she would have to do – the things he remembered her gushing about so excitedly that night before she had pinned him to the lounge and kissed him breathless – gave him a sick feeling of hope.

A lump formed in his throat, threatening to choke him when he tried to speak. “You’re not It, are you?” he croaked, entire body tensing like he was preparing for a hit.

Because Ginny was dead and that meant that Death could wear her face. Harry did not know if he could survive having her used against him like that.

“No,” Ginny said gently, kindly, and he wanted to believe her, but he felt like he was one wrong word away from unspooling.

“But you’re not Ginny either, are you?”

She did not answer, just continued to watch him with the same careful measure of fondness she had always held towards him.

Harry pressed his lips together, fingers digging into his arms harshly. “What is this? What the hell is going on?”

“You’re dreaming,” Ginny told him plainly. “You’re hurt and scared and so you burrowed deep down in an effort to hide because you can’t handle what happened.”

Harry stiffened, his jaw flexing at the implication. “I’m not hiding –”

“It wasn’t an insult,” she interrupted soothingly. “But it is the truth.”

Harry scoffed, defensive, and was approaching her without thought. “I got my throat cut open,” he spat, taking a tone he never normally would have with her. “I learned –” he swallowed.

“I know,” she said, the steadiness in her voice grounding him, stalling his anger before it could bubble forth. “You lost everything. You’re allowed to be upset. You’re allowed to be angry.”

“But?” Harry prompted, the word coming out unsteady. Ginny gestured for him to sit and against his better judgement, he did; sinking into the cushions close enough to feel the heat of her. His eyes slipped shut and he pressed the thumb of his left hand into his right palm until he could feel the bones shift.

Ginny moved, sliding closer, and her hand came up to cup his cheek. Harry froze at the touch, aching, as she dipped her fingers into his hair and drew her thumb tenderly along the skin beneath his eye. “It’s not safe for you here, Harry,” she said. “You need to wake up.”

“I don’t want to,” he confessed, pushing into her touch. It was not even a lie. Better to deal with whatever horrors haunted him here than face a reality where she and all the others were gone.

“I know,” she answered, tugging him until she could press their foreheads together. “But you’re not meant to wallow away here. You need to go back.”

Harry collapsed into her, head sliding to the junction of her neck and shoulder, lulled by her scent and soft touch. He felt muddled, on the verge of being sick and so very tired. His mind shied away from knowing what awaited him should he wake.

“I – can’t I be selfish just this once?” he mumbled into her skin. “There’s nothing there for me anymore. It’s all gone. You’re gone. How am I…how am I supposed to…”

Hands dragged up his back, one arm curling around his shoulder while the other buried itself in his hair again. “We’re gone,” she agreed sadly, “but you can make new bonds. New memories. This could be a gift.”

A bruising bark of laughter left him. Harry wrapped his arms around her waist, pulling her into his lap. She folded into him, her weight on him the most natural thing in the world. “A gift?” he repeated, incredulous at the thought even if it was so painfully Ginny. To be hopeful when hope was dead. “Can I return to sender?”

He heard her huff in amusement, felt it ruffle his hair, and burrowed in closer.

He had always loved that sound, and he knew that this was not Ginny – he knew it – but everything from her perfume to the warmth of her in his arms to the way she scratched at his scalp was familiar and he could not remember the last time he had been touched like this.

It had been weeks and it had been eons.

His tears finally got the best of him. Harry crushed his face further into her neck. “I miss you so much,” he breathed, voice tangled by his sorrow. “God, I miss you so much.”

“I know, I know,” Ginny said, holding him tightly and laying a kiss against his head. “Though we’re not really gone, you can speak to us whenever you want.”

But that just made him cry harder.

How cruel was life that being the Master of Death had done this to him, and yet those tools could give him a small sliver of his loved ones back?

“It won’t be the same,” he said wetly, pulling back to look at her. “It won’t make things better, or easier. This…this is still eternity we’re talking about. There’s no end. Even if I do…” make new bonds, but he was not ready to say the words, “it’ll still end up as just me and…It.”

Ginny brushed some of his tears away, smiling sweetly at him, as if he was precious and wonderful. “You don’t know that,” she told him, and continued before he could question her. “Your life has never been your own. Dumbledore. Voldemort. The Ministry. People have been planning around you since you were born, but now you have the freedom to do whatever you want. To learn whatever you want, go wherever you want. Help others. Be good or, hell, be evil. Live how you want.”

She kissed him, chaste, and Harry sighed into it. Tears still wet his cheeks and his mouth felt uncomfortably dry, but for just a moment he felt whole.

“You have all the time in the world,” she said as she pulled away. “Don’t deprive yourself of living, Harry.”

His hand rose to cup the side of her neck as his eyes fluttered open. He stared at her, frowning in consideration, letting his fingers trail over her face. “Are you sure you’re just a figment of my imagination?” he asked with a dim smile. “You can talk me around just like she could.”

Ginny’s lips curled impishly. She placed a hand flat against his chest.

“Find the stone and ask me yourself.”

And then she was gone, and the room was gone, and Harry was alone in the infinite darkness once more.

Utterly alone – until a hand shoved him forward and then he was falling.





OoO


Waking was not kind.

The room was empty when Harry’s eyes snapped open, thrust from the nothingness into a world filled with light and colour and magical signatures that scraped against his senses like blunt knives, cutting into his senses and gouging his flesh.

His throat was in agony.

Harry coughed, shoving himself up because he was about to gag. There was a loud ringing in his ears, shrill and obnoxious, and he hunched over to try and get away from it.

It was too much too much too much –

Someone burst into the room, their voice joining the cacophony of noise. Hands were on him, gently trying to rearrange him on the bed, and Harry twisted out from under them, leaning over the side of the bed and heaving. What splattered on the ground was more liquid than anything, and Harry did not stop until he was shivering.

He was pushed back, a wipe cleaning away the mess on his face and someone was speaking to him, low and urgent as cool, healing magic began to roll through him.

Harry blinked rapidly to clear the spots in his vision, squinting through the tears so he could make out the face of the man standing over him. There were other people in the background now, but they were moving too quickly for him to focus on.

“Can you hear me?” the man asked, not for the first time. Harry nodded weakly, coughing again when he tried to respond.

“Don’t talk,” he cautioned, keeping a hand on Harry’s shoulder as he motioned to someone else. “Get Mr. Black out of here and get me another calming draught.”

Sirius? Harry thought, head turning to look, but by the time he had managed the movement the door was already closing. Come back, he wanted to demand only to end up coughing once more.

The healer shushed him, magic rushing to Harry’s chest and easing the burning pain. “Don’t try to talk, Nathan. It’s still healing, you have to let it rest.”

Harry slumped back, staring up at the ceiling. He reached up to brush his fingers over his throat, though he was quickly intercepted. Harry squirmed, his confusion beginning to melt away bit by bit.

Hospital, he realised. The fight, Hogsmeade, his throat.

The wheeze he made sounded awful, a pathetic rasp of air that had even the healer wincing.

“You’re safe,” the man explained hurriedly, “you’re at St Mungo’s for treatment. No one will hurt you. You’re safe.”

But I’m not, Harry wanted to say. His other hand grabbed blindly at the man’s robes, tugging on it insistently.

“We’ll get you a potion,” the healer said resolutely, covering Harry’s hand with his own in some attempt at comfort. “We’ll explain everything once you’re calm.”

Harry shook his head, ignoring the way it made his neck twinge.

The door was thrown open and a woman handed a vial to the healer. Harry almost smacked it out of his hand but he was still too sluggish to resist when they worked to tip it past his lips.

The artificial calm descended over him swiftly.

“I can’t believe he’s awake,” the woman said in a hushed voice, helping to guide Harry back into his pillow. “That he survived – he’s so lucky.”

A few tears dripped down his numb cheeks, but the woman cooed and wiped them away.

I’m not, Harry thought. I’m really not.



Chapter End Notes

So Harry didn't have a great time this chapter, but when does he ever, let's be real.

Like I said, I'm a bit iffy on certain things in this chapter, but I hit all the plot points I wanted to, so that has to count for something. There was supposed to be a scene with Orion, but I'll rework it and have it as the opening scene in the next chapter. I just couldn't cram him in without messing with the flow too much, so we'll get a glimpse of our boy next time!

Thanks again for all your patience and support - I really appreciate it! For interest, my tumblr is open if you want to come along to discover theories, scream at me, discuss new snippets or get some behind the scenes commentary!





Chapter Twenty


Chapter Notes

Thanks for all the patience everyone! Sorry it took so long, I lost a lot of motivation to write this year, and have really struggled to produce something I'm proud of. I'm still not 100% keen on this chapter (actually quite dislike some portions, if I’m honest) but if I don't post it now I never will, so I figured to just bite the bullet and get it done.

Appreciate all the kind messages over the year, and all your support. I write for fun, and getting to share things with you all is a large part of why I managed to finish this chapter. Love you all!



See the end of the chapter for more notes



The hospital still smelt the same. Crisp, a little bitter, and sharp in a way that made his nose tingle – as if he were constantly on the verge of sneezing.

Orion had not missed it between his visits but putting up with a nauseating scent was a small sacrifice in the grand scheme of things. Driven by curiosity as much as he was, Orion knew he would endure far worse than an irritating smell if it got him even an inch closer to accomplishing his goals.

And his current goal, while admittedly still out of his reach, was certainly closer than ever.

All in good time, he thought with a soft exhale.

Keeping his narrow-eyed gaze fixed on the door across from him, Orion leaned more heavily against the section of wall he had claimed. His fingers drummed erratically along his thigh in a futile attempt to expel some of his churning energy.

The waiting was nothing new to him. He had been coming in regularly since he had been discharged; and had spent hours sitting beside an unconscious body. Hours spent tracking the gentle rise and fall of a chest, and staring at the plain white bandages that were wrapped around a thin, delicate throat.

All he had done for the better part of a week was wait. He was practically an expert at it by this point – could write essays on the merits of patience – but that newfound tolerance did not mean he was without frustration.

Knowing that the person he wanted to see more than anything was on the other side of that door, that they were separated by only a few centimetres of wood…it grated.

He might not have protested when the nurse had ushered him out of the room, smart enough to understand his presence would be a distraction, but he had not enjoyed being treated like some misbehaving pup that had gotten underfoot.

Or like a child overstepping his bounds, a disdainful voice whispered. It sounded similar enough to his mother’s sneering remarks that anger instinctively sparked inside Orion. He squashed it back, tucking it and the writhing emotions it invoked into a distant corner of his mind before it could do more than paint a scowl across his face.

Orion took a deep breath and gently knocked his head back against the wall.

This past week had been more fraught with tension than he had anticipated. Grimmauld Place had never been a comfortable home by any stretch of the imagination, not with the memory of Lucretia hanging over them like a shroud, but it had never been so suffocating before.

Before he had defied his mother. Before he had barely skirted the edges of his father’s leniency.

With his mother and he locked in a silent battle, and his father deliberately not taking a side, the three of them had been plunged into a frigid stalemate that perforated the very foundations of their house. Melania Black had never been one to accept disobedience, and having Orion – her son and now, only, child – so brazenly speak out against her own views had soured whatever fleeting affection she had once held for him.

He told himself that it did not hurt, even as the knife that was her disgust slid further into his soft underbelly every time they saw each other.

He told himself it was – that it would be – worth it.

Orion blinked rapidly to relieve the building burn in his eyes. He banished those thoughts and focussed on the wooden grain of the door to Nathan’s room. It was painted white, dull and flat where the age had set in and leeched it of its vibrance. Around the edges the coat had started to flake and peel, revealing the original brown.

Truly, this entire wing of St Mungo’s could do with a touch-up. It was dreary and worn and made being a patient here a mind-numbing experience.

Orion would know, he had stayed just two days in the ward and had forgotten what colour was.

He made a note to mention the poor state of the interior to his father. Even a small donation from the Black family coffers would bring new life to these walls and give those stuck here something other than never-ending whiteness to look at. Nathan’s stay would be substantially longer than his after all, and Orion could not have his friend dying of boredom before they released him back into the wild.

Plus, it never hurt to have the foremost hospital in the country feel indebted to you.

The door slid open without warning and Orion, whose attention had not wavered in the long minutes since he had been banished, straightened abruptly enough that his boot heel clicked loudly against the floor.

The nurse stepped out, closing the door behind her. There was a strange expression on her face, the shadow of sympathy cast over her eyes, one she hastily wiped away when she realised that he was watching her.

“Mr. Black,” she greeted, arms folding defensively in front of her stomach.

Orion tilted his head in what could barely pass for an acknowledgement. His eyes moved beyond her to the door. “Is he –”

“Oh, um,” her hands rose as if to stem his words. “I’m afraid I’m not allowed to discuss this with you,” she said, tongue tripping in her haste to speak. Her cheeks grew ruddy, embarrassment and apprehension staining her skin, though to her credit she did not back down.

Not many had the guts to deny a Black; fewer still had the nerves to cut one off mid-sentence. Orion almost respected her for it.

Almost.

He pressed his lips together, doing nothing to hide his displeasure. It festered in the silence between them until the woman’s gaze skittered away and she bid a swift retreat with a shallow nod. Orion watched her go, head swivelling to track her until she rounded the corner and disappeared.

He refrained from marching after her, but merely because the desire to see Nathan was stronger.

As that thought danced along the edge of his mind, the itch to wrench the door open rose like the tide inside him, threatening once more to crest the limits of his restraint. Only his reluctance to disturb the healer kept him pinned in place. That man had a quiet ferocity lurking beneath his soft exterior – the echoes of a temper that Orion was hesitant to poke at, especially now that Nathan was awake.

His arms crossed over his chest, and he breathed out slowly as his fingers dug into the fabric of his coat.

Nathan was awake.

Orion let that fact seep into him and enjoyed how the ground felt, for the first time in almost two weeks, solid underneath his feet.

Nathan was awake.

He released a long, deliberate sigh.

It had been a sudden thing, unexpected in the way miracles often were, but still an incredibly welcome change. When Orion had spotted the healers running in this direction, he had been suspicious; but when he heard them yelling the name Ciro…he had feared the worst.

Had feared that something had gone wrong.

He had no memory of following them, nor of shouldering his way into the room. Orion had not even realised what was happening until he had been standing a foot away from Nathan, watching the older boy lean over the side of his bed and throw up whatever potion concoction had been sitting in his stomach.

He did remember the absurd urge to laugh bubbling up inside him though.

It had not been a pretty scene to walk in on, but it had been a damn sight sweeter than the one of Nathan’s useless body laying limp and pale and dreadfully uninteresting on the mattress.

Now it was as if a weight had been lifted off his chest. No longer were there vines strangling and snatching his breath. Nathan was awake. He was aware. All he had to do was stay that way and perhaps Orion would finally have a moment’s peace.

He thought back over the last month and the sheer chaos Nathan had brought into his life; and allowed a small but pleased smile to creep forth. Orion had not been this interested, this engaged, since Riddle had started to play the game.

That magic, he mused, lightly biting his bottom lip and glancing up to trace his eyes along the bland ceiling.

A shiver hit him just at the memory of it. Those black wisps, the heaviness choking the air, that saccharine yet pungent taste of rotting fruit that seared itself into the back of his tongue…

Something unnatural had been there that day, Orion was sure of it. Beyond Nathan himself, something unknown and more had been on that street with them. It was a presence unlike anything he had ever felt before, and Orion wanted to know what. He craved the knowledge, wanted it with a desperation that was as unsettling as it was unsurprising.

He wanted it – and so he would have it.

Everyone always ended up telling him what he wanted to know, whether they realised it or not. Nathan would give him the truth eventually, even if Orion had to lie, trick, manipulate or scratch it out of him. And if a tiny part of him hoped it came to that, well, it was only because things would not be nearly as entertaining if the pieces simply fell into his lap.

The challenge, the chase, was half the fun.

He just had to wait a little longer.





OoO


Harry sat propped up in his hospital bed by an army of pillows and stared at the wall. His hands lay in his lap, fingers loosely curled in the sheet that was draped over his legs.

The warm haze that infected his mind was familiar in its cloying, possessive hold. It lingered, smothering every whisper of emotion – of anger, of confusion, of panic and sorrow and pain – that tried to claw to the forefront of his mind. He kept waiting for the flood to hit him, to be swept away by the hurricane that was brewing in him; but there was a wall between him and what he should be feeling, and no matter how Harry’s heart howled, it would not break.

He was quiet. Calm. Utterly disconnected from everything, unable to even muster the energy to turn his head or shift his limbs from where they had been arranged.

There was a riotous sea churning inside him, and Harry could not feel a drop of it.

His eyes fluttered closed, ignoring the conversation that was occurring above his head. It was difficult to concentrate on what was happening around him when the memories that played on the backs of his eyelids were so much more compelling.

Memories of Ron and Hermione, of little Teddy and brilliant, beautiful Ginny. Memories of his friends and colleagues, of work and home, of the war and the tent and Hogwarts and even the little muggle café that he stopped at most mornings for a pastry.

They washed over him, slow and sluggish, but strong. Harry remembered it all, remembered them all, but there was no pang of loss accompanying the thoughts.

He should be feeling something, he thought, peeling his eyes open to gaze down at his hands. He should be crying, sobbing, screaming about what was taken from him – yet there was nothing.

Everything and everyone he had ever loved was lost to him, and Harry could not mourn. His tears had dried up the moment the draught had touched his tongue.

He let himself drift instead of dwelling in that emptiness, trying to focus on the healer that orbited him. The man’s presence was comforting and recognisable, long established in Harry’s subconscious as safe, and his magic was soothing as it worked through his body.

Hands, warm and calloused, handled Harry as if he was a porcelain doll, until eventually the man came back around to stand in front of him. He tilted Harry’s head slightly to check the bandages around his neck, and those eyes were so full of concern and gentle care that Harry had to look away.

Another brush of healing magic sank into his throat, easing the pressure he had not even realised was building there, and Harry’s next breath came marginally easier. The spell continued for a long minute before the magic dissipated and the healer stepped back, pocketing his wand.

Harry studied that haggard face in the ensuing pause, watching the cycle of emotions flicking through his eyes, until the tension broke.

The healer blew out a tired breath, deep lines carving into his face. “Would you like some water?” he asked.

Harry parsed the question, then nodded when his exhausted mind deciphered the words. He was parched.

A cup with a straw was brought up to his mouth and a hand cradled the back of his skull. “Go slow,” the man cautioned, guiding the straw past his lips. Harry drank as best he could with the muscles in his neck twinging.

It was a strange sensation. The skin pulled at each swallow, and under the faint constrictions of the bandages there was an unfamiliar weight on the front of his neck that pressed in on his oesophagus. It made him want to gag.

He leaned back when his stomach gurgled in warning, and the cup was whisked out of sight the second he shifted.

“You’re alright, you’re okay,” he assured him, letting Harry drop back down onto the pile of pillows. “It’s just your muscles working underneath the scar tissue. It’ll feel wrong and a bit tight, but I swear you’re not going to choke.”

Harry swallowed once more, deliberately, just to feel the strangeness again; then looked down and flexed his fingers carefully. He still felt heavy, so he pressed the tip of each finger against his thumb, one after another. They followed his instructions without delay, as if they had always been his.

“You haven’t had anything solid since you were brought in, so you’ll feel a bit off until we get some food in you,” the healer said, grabbing the clipboard from the end of Harry’s bed and writing a few quick notes on the paperwork. He worked in silence, and only once he was done did he meet Harry’s eyes.

There was a thick layer of discomfort on his face, but his voice was genuine when he spoke. “It’s good to see you awake. My name is Martin Turner, and I’ve been overseeing your treatment while you’ve been here.”

Turner gave him a nice attempt at a smile. “You’ve been here for ten days. We’ve been keeping you under to give your body time to heal. We stopped administering the sleeping potions this morning, but we weren’t expecting you to wake up so soon.” Quick, clever eyes darted down to Harry’s neck, and that smile grew a touch more fixed.

“Do you remember what happened, Mr. Ciro?”

Harry blinked, and one of his hands slid up to touch the thick wrappings around his throat. He had no problem tracing the path the cutting curse had made, and that small motion brought with it the sense memory of hot blood pouring between his fingers and down his chest. Of snow and the buzz of battle magic and –

“See you around, Master.”

His throat clicked as he swallowed again, and he nodded.

Turner nodded back, seeming relieved that he would not have to be the one to explain. His hands fluttered along the clipboard, and his eyes darted to the door. “I should wait for your parents to arrive before we go further,” he said, though there was a hint of reluctance in his tone. “One of the nurses has gone to alert them, so it shouldn’t be long.”

The idea of having to wait for Cynthia and Benedict – not his parents, never his parents – to hear his own medical information would have annoyed him if Harry were capable of feeling anything. He had not been a child in a very long time and did not need or want people to start coddling him now.

He reached out and laid a hand on Turner’s arm, staring up at him with silent demand.

Turner twitched at the touch, glancing down at Harry’s hand with a frown. Harry opened his mouth, wetting his lips – but Turner jolted forward and waved at him to stop. “No,” he said forcefully, “no talking. You can’t stress your vocal cords.”

Harry cocked his head, and he had no idea what his face was conveying but it made Turner look down at the floor with a sad curl to his mouth. The man grimaced as his eyes flicked back towards the door. His shoulders rose up to his ears as he was consumed by his thoughts, before they dropped down into a defeated slump.

He caved so easily it was clear he had never wanted to wait in the first place.

“Very well,” he muttered, pushing his fringe back. Turner pulled the bedside seat closer so that they were face-to-face and braced the clipboard over his thighs. He steepled his fingers and stared at Harry, grim but still, somehow, impossibly kind.

“During the attack, your throat was severed,” he began, and his frank honesty would have been confronting were Harry not used to healers and their blunt-force approach to discussing injuries. “The curse cut across your larynx – your vocal cords,” he clarified, “but it also affects your breathing and your ability to swallow. Your artery was cut as well, which caused you to lose a lot of blood.”

Turner stopped briefly, perhaps to give Harry time or because he himself needed to collect his composure.

“From our observations over the past few days, your use of flash healing repaired the internal damage, and the structure of your throat is as it should be.” Something dark flashed over his face as he leaned forward to hold Harry’s gaze. “However, the sheer amount of magic you pushed into those muscles will have a lasting impact. They’re strained, Nathan. Very strained. The physical damage might have been largely reversed but the trauma still happened, and the effects will remain for a while. You need to let the muscles rest, which means no talking.”

Under the influence of the calming draught, Harry only needed a few moments to process that. He turned the information over in his mind with clinical interest, before settling into bland acceptance. Turner watched him carefully, but carried on when all Harry did was look back at him.

“Do you understand, Nathan? What you did…I’ve never seen flash healing applied like this. Neck injuries can be difficult to recover from, and I can’t guarantee anything.” Turner spread his hands, his head shaking a little in a show of disbelief. “I can only go off what I know of similar instances of trauma to the neck – hangings and crush injuries and the like. What you endured…it’s not an injury people usually walk away from. We’ll need to take this slow. It will be frustrating, and stressful. You’ll have dietary restrictions, physical and speech therapy, and –”

Turner bit his lip. “And I think you should see someone to help you process things.”

It was not the first time Harry had heard that. Everyone from Hermione to McGonagall to Dudley during the one face-to-face meeting they had had after he had left the Dursleys had told Harry to talk to someone about what he had gone through.

About his childhood, and the war, and Voldemort, and dying.

Harry had never quite found the time, and he knew that that was not about to change.

“The coming months will be hard, and I’ve no doubt that once the draught has run its course you will feel overwhelmed. Your scar will be very prominent, and a lot of things will change for you because of it.”

A prominent scar. Harry almost wished he had the capacity to laugh.

He brushed his fingers over his throat again, paying no heed to the sympathy that bled into Turner’s face.

“And Nathan…if – when you regain your speech, there’s a high chance you won’t sound the same,” he told him, lowering his voice to soften the blow. “The scar tissue is pressing against your larynx, and that pressure will change the way air passes through your vocal cords. It will likely be…upsetting to hear yourself.”

With that last piece said, Turner slouched back into his seat, drained.

Harry stared forward, his thoughts clicking away, before closing his eyes.

That was that then.

The last remnant of Nathan Ciro was gone.

Over the weeks, ever since he had been dumped in this body, everything had become his. The way he talked, the way he walked, the way he dressed, and acted and brushed his hair and held his wand – it had all been overwritten by Harry.

But the voice, the unique sound of it, that had remained Nathan’s. Higher, naturally softer, and far more polished than Harry’s own voice had ever been. It had been a constant, unsettling reminder that his body did not belong to him, even if it had become easier to ignore.

But now that too was erased because of him. Because he had not been fast enough, strong enough, to avoid the cut.

Harry’s recklessness had destroyed the last traces of this young boy, and no one knew the depth of the loss but him.

Turner had made a mistake revealing this all while he was under a calming draught. The man had likely thought it would make it easier to handle, that he was giving Harry time to understand the situation without the weight of the emotional turmoil.

All he had done was delay the inevitable.

I’m going to have a breakdown, Harry thought with jarring tranquillity. I’m going to explode and bring this entire hospital wing down with me.

Too much had happened in too short a time. He was standing on a precipice and the minute his emotions returned, he was going to tip right over the edge.

Harry looked down at his tiny, pale hands and realised with that same awful, disturbing clarity –

I’m going to get someone killed.





OoO


It could not have been more than half an hour when Orion heard voices and footsteps – loud and harried, suggesting more than one person – approaching rapidly from down the hallway. His eyes snapped over just in time to see the nurse and three others, one of whom was all too familiar, come rushing around the corner.

Orion let out a soft hum.

The Ciros had arrived. Father, mother, son – all come to visit their beloved bastard.

Well, he amended, gaze lingering on Simon, mostly beloved.

Mouth curling into a brief, disdainful smile, Orion shifted to stand properly. He tracked them with eager eyes, making sure to arrange his expression into something appropriately sympathetic and welcoming as they reached him.

It was Simon who recognised him first, those flat blue eyes blowing wide in shock as his steps stuttered. Beneath the flood of emotions there was also, Orion was amused to note, a dash of genuine fear in the other’s reaction. It seemed disproportionate given he had never really played with Simon enough to foster that level of caution, but it did show his rising understanding of the situation they found themselves in.

After all, Orion being here outside Nathan’s room well before even his family was as explicit a statement as he could make without saying the bloody words.

He held Simon’s stare for a moment, watching the bubbling panic while vowing to actually do something to earn that delightful fear, before turning his attention to Lady Ciro.

The poor woman was frazzled, distracted by thoughts of her injured child. She looked unkempt with her blonde hair falling around her shoulders, dress rumpled from where her hands had clutched at it, and those dark circles bruising the skin under her eyes. Unlike his own mother, there was no pretence behind Cynthia Ciro’s mien, and Orion thought she wore her distress and concern rather well.

Instead of diminishing her beauty it, somehow, elevated it. Made her seem real.

There was a small delay in her noticing him, but when her eyes did finally land on him everything about her sharpened into a threat so subtle and smooth that it took Orion by surprise. People – adults, really – did not normally grow wary of him until at least the second encounter, and that was only if he was not trying to restrain himself.

“Lady Ciro,” he said politely, offering a deeper bow than strictly necessary given their difference in station; though considering he intended to rely on her good graces shortly, it made sense not to alienate her.

Orion watched her from under his lashes as he also greeted her husband.

“Orion Black,” Benedict murmured, sounding more confused than anything. The man’s heavy brows were furrowed, the stress of the last few months having made a home on his face. It aged him terribly, marring his otherwise handsome features with wrinkles.

Cynthia was far more critical in her appraisal, softened though it was due to his unexpected show of respect. She scanned him closely, her mouth pinching with unsaid things, before she decided his presence was less important than her child’s wellbeing. She dismissed him out of hand, turning instead to the nurse.

“My son?” she asked with tightly restrained desperation; and Cynthia might not have shared a drop of blood with Nathan, but she was every bit as expressive as he was.

Learned behaviour, Orion assumed, continuing to observe with silent fascination.

“The healer should be finished with the examination. I’ll let him know you’re here,” the witch said with a kind smile, stepping away to knock on the door. For a moment it seemed as if Cynthia would follow, but her husband cupped her elbow and stopped her before she could move.

The two shared a long look, an entire discussion happening in the span of a heartbeat, before Cynthia settled into the circle of Benedict’s arms. She tucked her head under his chin, grasping at his hands and sinking into the steady expanse of his chest. Benedict, for his part, pressed a kiss to the crown of her head and whispered something in her ear that had her releasing a shuddering breath.

Orion could never imagine his parents showing such a degree of emotion in public. It was almost enough to make him avert his eyes.

Fortunately, the nurse moved back from the door, holding it open so the healer could exit, and all thoughts of the embarrassing display fled his mind.

Cynthia wasted no time on pleasantries. “Nathan, is he…?”

The healer – Martin Turner, if Orion recalled correctly – nodded tiredly. He looked like he had not slept in a week, but his eyes were keen and alert. “Nathan is awake, and he’s cognisant. I had to administer a calming draught, but it’ll work its way out of his system soon enough.”

Nathan’s parents deflated in relief. Benedict clutched at his wife’s shoulders, his head bowing forward as his eyes squeezed shut. “May we see him?” he asked, voice a wretched whisper. Tears wet his lashes when he looked up.

Turner smiled, ushering them towards the door. “Of course. We can talk inside. There is a lot to discuss going forward.”

Though Cynthia’s expression creased with the beginnings of exhaustion she entered the room without an ounce of hesitation, Benedict at her heels. The two of them were so single-minded in their need to see one son that they appeared to forget the other. Only Turner offered a vaguely questioning glance at Simon when he made no effort to follow, before he slipped in after them and closed the door.

Orion eyed the door sullenly, hating that propriety had once again gotten in his way, then shifted his focus to the nurse. This time, she bowed to his demands without a hint of regret or protest, and left quietly. He waited until she was far enough away before turning to face his schoolmate.

His tongue darted out along his bottom lip, marvelling at his own luck.

“Not going in?”

Simon jolted, ripped from his reverie and immediately off balance as a result. His eyes did a quick lap of the hallway, and when he found them alone his swallow was audible.

“I – pardon?”

Orion bobbed his head towards the door, leaning back against the wall and crossing his arms. Compared to the rigid stance of Simon, he was the picture of relaxation. “Aren’t you going to go in? It’s only family at the moment, unfortunately, but that shouldn’t stop you.”

A flurry of emotions danced over Simon’s face, eventually settling on a twisted kind of envy. “No, I’m not. They don’t – they don’t need me in there.”

“Who said anything about need?” Orion asked with a guileless smile. “Don’t you want to see your brother?”

Yes, every inch of Simon’s body seemed to scream – but he was smart enough to not admit that aloud to Orion.

“Why are you even here?” Simon demanded after he let the pause stretch too far, puffing up with indignation. It did nothing to hide how his gaze roamed along the hall again, or how that fear from earlier came bludgeoning back to the surface, eating away at the last vestiges of Simon’s self-control.

Orion blinked at him, intrigued at how someone as ruthless and self-serving as this boy could, at the same time, be so easily shaken. The dichotomy was so strong that he had to wonder how Simon even functioned day-to-day. Surely the other boy would drive himself mad trying to align those two aspects of himself.

“I’m here to check up on my friend,” he answered calmly, head swaying to the side as he waited. When Simon finally found the nerve to meet his eyes once more, Orion let his mouth curl up meanly, and struck. “Why are you?”

The question hit hard, cracking against Simon’s flimsy handle on his emotions. Orion received the distinct pleasure of watching the colour flee the other’s face as he stood there trembling, reeling, as if the world were collapsing around him because of those three little words.

“He…he’s my brother.”

The response was weak, warbling, and Orion cocked an unimpressed eyebrow. “And yet you refuse to see him?”

He straightened from his indolent slouch, feeling every bit the stalking predator. As he watched Simon take a tiny step back, derision sloshed inside him – it messily covered his amusement, staining it an ugly shade, and burned along the roof of his mouth when he continued.

“My my, how quickly you sing a different tune. You don’t need to pretend in front of me, Simon. I’ve heard it all already.” Orion leaned in conspiratorially, teeth carefully tucked away, as if sharing a joke. “We both know that if you really thought that you wouldn’t be out here talking to me.”

Gods, he looked like he was about to cry.

It was too easy sometimes for Orion. Easy to sink his claws in and rip their tender underbellies open. Easy to spill their guts and pick and prod and feast on their weaknesses to his own content.

But even Simon’s reaction, while nice, was not enough. Orion had scented the blood in the water, and now the need to draw more had him in a chokehold.

He moved closer.

“I mean, we all knew you hated him,” Orion let out a soft chuckle, “given how quickly those rumours spread, and how little you seemed to care that your brother was being torn to shreds right in front of you. But looking at you now…” He blatantly raked his gaze over Simon, from his teary eyes to the flighty shifting of his feet. “It’s like you can’t stand the idea of him being hurt.”

Quick as a viper, Orion closed the gap between them; inserting himself right into Simon’s space to breathe a secret into his ear.

“But that’s because his pain only satisfies when you’re the one behind it, right?”

Simon recoiled, a full-bodied retreat that was as damning as a confession.

A thin, mirthless smile slashed across Orion’s face.

He had suspected for a while, ever since the details around Nathan’s dive off a Knockturn Alley roof had reached him, because Simon was just that breed of spiteful, yet painfully myopic, asshole.

The events at Hogsmeade and the overheard conversation between Nathan and who must have been his assaulters had merely brought that niggling suspicion roaring to the forefront. Orion had never said anything, never even hinted at the possibility, because he had never cared enough to voice the accusations.

But now?

Oh, now he was involved.

Now he was interested.

Now he cared – and that was all the justification Orion needed for what he was about to do.

“I didn’t – I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Simon croaked, his words hitching halfway through. The realisation of how precarious his position was right now lit up his eyes like wildfire.

“Oh, I think you do,” Orion replied, hands folding neatly behind his back as he stared up at Simon. “See, I watch people. What they do, what they say, what they don’t say…I study it all. All in good fun, of course, just a way to pass the time – but it means I know things, Simon.”

He paused and took a breath, thought back to Nathan’s prone body and red snow, and then let loose.

“Like the fact that you’ve always been an insecure little boy. Desperate for attention, for affection, for recognition for your meagre accomplishments. I know that it didn’t matter so much to you when you thought you were twins – Nathan getting so much love from your parents. He was your other half, an extension of yourself, so any attention he got was basically yours by default.”

He clicked his tongue playfully, rocking back on his heels. “But when you found out the truth? When you realised he wasn’t your mother’s son? Seeing them dote and fawn over him, seeing her doing it…that must have itched. Suddenly you weren’t competing with a twin. No, suddenly it was this boy from your father’s sordid affair. Suddenly it was your father’s shame encroaching on your territory. And poor little Simon just couldn’t swallow the injustice anymore.”

Simon opened his mouth, but Orion was not done. He continued to give voice to all the dark little thoughts he knew the other boy had nursed.

“Your brother was no longer your twin, rather a bastard and an older one at that; but that didn’t matter a whit to your parents. Worse still, they had clearly known this entire time and had been lying to you.” Orion inhaled through his teeth, wincing in mock sympathy. “Betrayal abounds. So you, pathetic cretin that you are, started to stew and simmer and seethe, and then you started to think it wasn’t fair.”

“That’s not –”

“Nevermind that Nathan had been lied to as well. Nevermind that his world had just shattered right alongside yours. You decided it wasn’t fair that he got to keep all the love and attention and the Ciro name when he never should have had it in the first place. That he only crept into your family because the trollop your father fucked had died and your parents were too soft-hearted to leave him out in the cold.”

“Stop it -”

Orion ignored him.

“So you started scheming, wanting some form of retribution for the pain and humiliation you felt at being tricked for so long – and what better way to get that than to whisper the truth in the ears of those at Hogwarts? To incite the very conservative, very traditional portion of our society to tear into your unsuspecting brother?”

He clapped his hands together – once, twice, three times – in slow appreciation. “I’ll give it to you, you had Slytherins pegged from the beginning. Statistically the House with the highest concentration of purebloods, the ones most likely to take offence to Nathan’s very existence and make it loudly known. All you had to do was sit back and watch his Housemates cannibalise him. It was actually a bit clever.”

Orion would have admired the sheer pettiness that guided Simon’s actions, admired how simplistically brilliant the whole plan was, if his pride did not prickle at having his House used so blatantly as a hammer to shatter someone he now liked.

He reached out, fingers tucking under the lapels of Simon’s frock coat, and gently tugged the older boy closer so they stood chest to chest. Keeping his voice low, friendly if not for the poison coating his tongue, he continued, “But even with all that done, even with Nathan broken down and fading away, you still decided it wasn’t enough. That Nathan hadn’t been punished enough. You had destroyed his reputation, his future, his few genuine friendships, his peace of mind, his sense of self…what else could you possibly ruin?”

Orion fisted his hands, holding Simon right where he wanted him, and finally showed his teeth.

“Now, admittedly, this next bit is conjecture on my part, but I still think I’ll hit the mark. You’re not terribly difficult to figure out, after all.”

He could see the dilated pupils in Simon’s eyes, could practically smell the fear that flooded him, and knew he just needed one more push to come crashing down.

Orion was happy to oblige.

“You got in contact with the Acolytes.”

Those damning words dropped like a stone between them, and Simon went white.

“I don’t know how, I don’t know when, and I certainly don’t know why they agreed to your suggestion or deal or whatever – but I do think that it was you who pointed them your brother’s way. I think you told them all about your family’s tradition of attending Samhain celebrations in Diagon Alley. And I think you ensured your brother walked off alone that night.”

This close, breathing the same air, Orion could feel how Simon’s tremors morphed into frantic squirms. He was like an animal caught in a trap, seconds from gnawing off its own limb to escape.

“I think you thought they would just beat him, humiliate him, rough him up.” He hauled Simon in when he made to break away, refusing to let him go without one final dig. “I think the truth of what they did to him, what Nathan did in response, what you pushed him to do, actually terrified you. And do you know why I think all this?”

Simon released a short sob, catching his bottom lip to hold back the sound, and gave a tiny shake of his head.

“Because you’re a craven and a worm, and you don’t have the guts to be a murderer.”

Orion shoved him away, wiping his hands on his jacket as if to clean the filth off. Simon stumbled over his own feet and tripped into the wall. He clutched at the railing, pressing his face into the cold plaster to try and hide from the condemnation.

The sight of his cowardice had Orion gritting his teeth.

This was, perhaps, the thing that annoyed him the most. Orion could have respected Simon if only he owned what he had done. He might not have liked him, or agreed with his methods, or supported his choices, but Orion would have at least respected Simon’s conviction.

Instead, when the time came and he had to face what he had done, Simon flinched.

“You made others do your dirty work – made others torment Nathan, harass him, rape him – all so you could pretend your hands were clean. But the moment things escalated, the moment it got real for you and the consequences came knocking, you folded like wet parchment. Killing Nathan metaphorically was fine by you, but literally? Oh, you couldn’t handle it literally.”

He stopped there, cutting himself off and biting down on the other things he longed to say because this – this was quickly becoming dangerous. Unplanned confrontations were always a risk, and with the kinds of accusations Orion was throwing around, having this conversation in the middle of a hospital hallway was straying swiftly towards stupid. There were better places to fully unload onto Simon, at a time when his pulse was not racing, soaring with vindication, and clouding his judgement.

Orion slipped his hands into his pockets to cover how they shook from adrenaline.

He did feel unmoored in the best way though, satiated by the wounds he had so obviously inflicted, even if it was not nearly the recompense that Nathan deserved.

“H-how? How did you…?” Simon could not get the words out, choking on his tears.

Despite his disgust, Orion smiled pleasantly. “I told you I watched people. You were so desperate to be the victim, to have someone – anyone – sympathise with you that you asked the entirety of our society to weigh in on your family drama. Anything I observed or uncovered was only possible because you issued the invitation in the first place.”

Simon cowered against the wall like a beaten dog, his face blotchy and wet as he stared, devastated, down at the tiles. His mouth opened and closed soundlessly, unable to summon even a basic defence. He likely never thought to have one. Never thought that anyone would care enough to notice what he had done.

Nathan, even at his lowest, had never managed to look this pitiful.

“For someone so adept at stabbing people in the back,” Orion said quietly, voicing the observation without thought, “you really can’t handle the knife being turned on you, can you?”

That, after everything, seemed to be the final straw. Simon’s head snapped up, his eyes glowing with rage as his clenched fists shook at his sides.

“Why do you even care?” he snarled, though noticeably he remained at a near-whisper, ever mindful of his parents on the other side of the door. Simon took a daring step forward. “You never gave a single shit about Nathan before all this! You stood to the side just like everyone else and laughed at him! You were never his friend. You never helped him. You never defended him. But now you think you have the right to do it? To judge me?”

Orion raised his eyebrows, amused by the hypocrisy. “Your attempts to draw parallels between your piss poor treatment of Nathan and my past lack of care is flawed in several ways, none so obvious as this,” he jerked his chin towards the door. “He’s not my brother.”

“That doesn’t –”

“I had no obligation to be nice to Nathan,” Orion said. “I had no obligation to defend him or shield him or hold his hand through the damage you wrought. There was nothing between us that demanded that level of consideration from me.” His expression cooled into something truly threatening, enough that Simon hurriedly moved back.

“But that changes now.”

Dread was too light a word to describe the expression on Simon’s face. “What are you going to do?” he asked, gasping the words out. His eyes flicked back to the door, and that dread turned into a sickly type of guilt.

Orion debated not answering, of leaving Simon in the dark to marinate in his own worst fears, before deciding that the truth would be far more horrifying.

“I’m going to tell your brother everything, and then I’m going to let him decide what to do with you.”



Chapter End Notes

Hope you all liked it!
