Chapter Twenty-one


Chapter Notes

Thanks for your patience everyone! Please enjoy the chapter, and let me know what you think!



See the end of the chapter for more notes



He can’t.

The thought speared through Simon as his stomach dropped to his knees.

He can’t. He can’t. He can’t.

But he could.

There was no deceit or indecisiveness on Black’s face as he stared Simon down; only a placid smile set below malicious eyes that practically dared him to call his bluff. To give him the excuse he wanted to bring everything crashing to the ground.

This was…it was insane.

Black was thirteen, a full year younger than Simon, yet he held himself as assuredly as a man three times his age while brandishing his threats. Looking at him right now – straight-backed and with a confident, superior tilt to his chin – it was all too easy to imagine him out on the floor of the Wizengamot, conducting the high courts and elite pureblood representatives like a maestro would a choir.

Simon, swallowing back the sudden surge of bile that boiled in his throat, grappled with the uncomfortable tightness rising in his chest.

Being in another year and House, he had never had much exposure to Black before. They ran in different circles, had different interests and hobbies, and beyond the few stilted exchanges they had shared, their lives had never intersected. The two of them might as well have existed on separate planets, and that distance also meant he had never given much credence to the rumours surrounding the other boy.

Nor the nervous smiles and averted eyes that seemed to dog Black’s steps.

Or how a single look from him could quell the boldest of their peers.

Or the fact that no one – not the older students, not the professors, not the hospital staff or even Simon’s own parents – appeared keen to challenge him.

The cautionary tales that clung to Black had always sounded too outlandish for Simon to take them seriously. He distinctly remembered scoffing more than once at the label, the title, that he sometimes caught whispers of from his classmates when Black was around, but it was all he could hear now, drowning out even the frantic drumming of his heart.

Wizarding royalty.

Orion Cygnus Black came from old stock after all, able to trace his family roots with pinpoint accuracy back to France in the Middle Ages. It was that lineage, with generations upon generations of precise breeding in a long and uninterrupted bloodline, that made him a prince in all but name.

It was what had eyes following him since the moment he set foot on Hogwarts’ hallowed grounds. What had crowds parting for him and people quietening out of respect when he spoke. What had everyone, from the lowest house-elves to some of the most venerated members of their society, deferring to a child.

So many dreamed of associating with Black, of holding his attention for even a minute, that they were willing to do damn near anything for the chance.

And now this boy, with his political capital and immense social influence and caverns of wealth so deep the goblins of Gringotts salivated over them, had aligned himself with Nathan. This boy – thirteen, and already so certain of his place in the world, in his inherent worth – had openly declared himself Nathan’s friend in the same breath he promised to tear Simon’s life apart; and it was terrifying to know how outclassed he was.

Terrifying, and infuriating because all he wanted to know was why?

Why Nathan?

Why was all that power standing behind Nathan, bolstering Nathan, protecting Nathan?

What had his brother – his meek, unassuming, trusting brother – done to earn such dedication?

Envy, familiar in its poisonous warmth, began to bubble in Simon’s veins along with a new spike of fear.

There would be nothing left for him once Nathan knew the truth. Once their parents knew, he corrected numbly, because there was no way this would stop with his brother.

Even if Nathan, with his strange and newly confrontational attitude, chose not to tell mother and father what he had done Simon knew that Black would, if only to ensure everything he had been clinging to was wrenched away and his greatest fears manifested.

His mother’s horror. His father’s disgust.

No, he thought, shaking his head to clear the despondent clouds that filled it. No, it can’t end like this. I can’t let him do this.

But what could he do to stop it? What could he offer a boy that already had everything?

Black watched him, avidity and biting humour gleaming in his grey eyes as Simon spiralled, desperately hunting for an answer, for anything he might give to get the other boy to reconsider – yet nothing he came up with seemed to be a viable way out.

Frustrated tears prickled at the corners of his eyes, and hopelessness strangled him.

He had never meant for things to go as far as they had. He had never meant for Nathan to be hurt like he had been. Simon had just gotten so caught up in himself – in his pain and anger, in the loneliness and heartache, and the heady rush of control he felt after being powerless for so long – that he had not been able to stop.

Every time he tried to take that step back, to restrain the awfulness that was inside him, something always happened to reignite his rage.

Like the day Nathan had sworn to renounce any inheritance in favour of Simon – crushed when their parents refused to take Nathan off their will.

Or the time Nathan had broken down in front of him – spoiled by how he pretended the next day that nothing had happened, that he had not begged on his knees for Simon to not hate him.

Or the long nights after Nathan had tried to take his own life, when all Simon could do was stare up at the ceiling and drown in regret – tainted by Nathan waking with no memories, unburdened by the truth that had wrecked their family and acting as if he were untouchable.

It had all just been so unfair, how nothing ever seemed to go his way, but even in his darkest moments Simon had never wanted Nathan dead.

“Because you’re a craven and a worm, and you don’t have the guts to be a murderer.”

Black was right. He was right about it all.

Simon could never be a murderer, not when he could barely acknowledge what he had done without the paralysing guilt gnawing at his core.

Not that any of that would matter. Once word got out, once Black told people, Simon would be ruined. The same people that had picked at Nathan’s open wounds would flock to him, ready for the next feast.

And the only one that might save him had the least reason to.

He wet his lips and cast his pride aside. “Please,” he said, “please don’t –”

The click of the door opening had him closing his jaw so quickly he nearly bit through his tongue. The rest of his plea curdled in his mouth.

Simon’s eyes jumped to the side, foolishly taking his focus away from the boy in front of him, and watched as his mother and the healer stepped into the hallway. They were mid-conversation, but his father was nowhere to be seen.

His heart lurched, slamming up into the base of his throat.

Had she overheard?

His mother continued speaking in low tones to the healer, her words too soft to make out, and she reached out to take one of the man’s hands in hers. Her gratitude was evident in her tremulous smile and earnest eyes. The stress that had made a home in her spine over the past week was not gone, but there was an easement in her shoulders that made a corner of him relieved, even if the reason for it was Nathan.

While she was preoccupied, he darted a look back at Black and immediately blanched when he saw how intently the other was staring at his mother. Sensing Simon’s attention, those steely eyes slid over to meet his and a tiny, goading smirk slithered onto Black’s lips.

No. No. No, no, nonono.

In a brash attempt to shield his mother from the other boy’s plots, Simon rushed to her side. He wanted to cover her ears, to prevent her from hearing any truths that could be spilled, to preserve the love she had for him, but she turned before he could do more than grasp her elbow.

Instantly, some of his terror faded. There was nothing but tender affection on her face when she looked at him.

“Sweetheart,” Cynthia said, glancing away long enough to nod in farewell to the healer, then her hand came up to cup his cheek. “You didn’t want to go in?” she asked gently, and the question stuck under his skin like a splinter.

Simon understood her confusion.

When the firecall had come through to let them know that Nathan was awake, he had all but flung himself into the floo to get to the hospital. His anxiety had been genuine, all-consuming, and clearly what his parents had expected.

But once they arrived, once they were walking down the sterile hallways and it all became real, that want to see his brother had shrivelled up until the very idea of stepping into his room made Simon want to be sick.

Not that staying out here with Black had been any better.

Simon chanced another look at the boy, and smothered a wince at the unblinking stare that was fixed on both of them. He faced his mother hurriedly to avoid being caught in that gaze.

“I just…I couldn’t,” he said, choking down his shame as she gave him an understanding smile.

“That’s alright, darling. I know it hurts,” she smoothed some of his fringe back, tucking the longer strands behind his ear. “Your brother is going to be fine, though,” Cynthia said with the firm belief of a mother that would do everything in her power to make her words come true. “It will take some time, but everything will work out.”

It was exactly what she had told him the last time he had been admitted, when the ground of Knockturn Alley was still wet with Nathan’s blood. The only difference this time was that it was not Simon and his hasty actions behind his brother’s injuries.

“That’s good,” he said, trying to return her smile. “Where is father?”

Cynthia’s face fell into misery. “He wanted a few more minutes with Nathan. He’ll be along shortly, and we’ll be returning to the manor to collect some clothes for your brother. The ones he had on were…destroyed, and Healer Turner believes it might be better for Nathan if he wore something of his own instead of the hospital gowns.”

To her credit, the hitch in her words was subtle enough that Simon only heard it because he was standing so close. He squeezed her arm to try and console her and wondered morbidly if his mother had seen Nathan’s clothes after they had been stripped from him. If that was what put such a haunted sheen in her eyes as she spoke of it.

“We’ll return this afternoon, once we’ve all had a chance to process.” Cynthia bit her lip, visibly debating her next words, then she sighed. “If you want to remain at the manor instead of coming back, that’s alright. Your father and I know that seeing Nathan like this is difficult, so if you want to wait until he’s been discharged, we understand.”

Her hand fell to his shoulder, clutching it lightly, before she finally turned to address their unwanted audience. His mother’s expression cooled, all her warmth and vulnerability carefully folded away as she offered a regal dip of her head. “Orion Black,” she greeted, “forgive me for not acknowledging you. I was concerned with my son’s health.”

It was a superfluous apology in Simon’s eyes. Black was the interloper here, and if he had had a scrap of decency, he would have made himself scarce the moment they had arrived.

But the gods were not that kind.

There was no sign of Black’s earlier aggression or unusual interest. His teeth were hidden and claws sheathed when he smiled at Cynthia – though the memory of their sharp sting would linger in Simon’s mind. He shifted closer to his mother, remaining half a step behind her in the perfect position to observe Black without giving anything away.

His fingers were still curled around Cynthia’s elbow, flexing. The last thing he wanted was her talking with the person holding a match over the oil spill that was his life and reputation, but his hands were tied right now.

“It’s perfectly alright, Lady Ciro,” Black said, waving her apology away with an amiability that, to Simon’s horror, had his mother thawing slightly. “I know that Nathan is your priority.”

The insult flew over his mother’s head, but it sunk into Simon like meat hooks. Instead, she focussed on the familiarity with which Black said Nathan’s name.

“Are you well acquainted with my sons, Mr. Black?” It was not quite innocent curiosity that coated his mother’s voice, but whether she was laying some kind of verbal snare or not, Simon could not tell.

Black stepped into it regardless, utterly unafraid. “I’m not close with Simon, but Nathan and I are Housemates and, more importantly, friends.”

“Really?” Cynthia countered, managing to sound remorseful at her lack of knowledge, rather than doubtful. “He’s never spoken of you.”

“It’s a very recent development,” Black amended, his eyes crinkling as his smile deepened. Everything about him softened in the next instance, a touch of hesitance creeping into his tone, and his words hit Cynthia like a weapon. “I was with Nathan at Hogsmeade.”

That admission, and the quiet, calculative downward curl of his lips had any remnants of caution draining out of Cynthia. Her hands dropped to her sides and her chin lowered. “Yes,” she said, sympathy coating her words, “I remember the aurors mentioning that.”

She glanced at the floor, and the moment she took her eyes off him, Black shot Simon a look that was purely a taunt. It had him shifting backwards instinctively. His fingers twitched, longing for the comforting weight of his wand.

Cynthia saw none of it.

“They told me that you stayed with him. That he only survived long enough to receive proper aid because you and the other boys watched over him.”

Black’s expression shuttered, all emotion wiped clean in an instant. It surprised Simon, but whatever thoughts prompted the abrupt change were impossible to guess.

“We did stay with him,” Black confirmed.

“And…are you well?” his mother asked, eyes gliding over Black in search of wounds that would have been tended to and healed days ago. “I heard there were injuries.”

“I am, thank you,” Black said evenly. “I was hurt during the raid, but Nathan defended me.” The other boy paused, then continued solemnly, “He’s the only reason I survived the attack.”

And that – that made Simon freeze.

Oh shit, he thought hollowly.

This was worse than just a friendship. Worse than curiosity or twisted entertainment.

This was Black indebted to Nathan, and if he had decided that the payment for saving his life was information, then nothing Simon said or did or offered would dissuade him.

His vision swam, blood roaring in his ears.

How? How was Nathan able to save Black in a way that would warrant a fucking debt? His brother had never been impressive as a duellist – not when compared to someone like Black who would have been privately tutored by professionals from the time he could hold a wand.

It was laughable to think, but why would Black admit to it if it did not happen? The other boy was prideful, and he would never allow such a rumour to spread unless it was real and somehow benefited him to have it known.

Simon’s eyes snapped between his mother and Black, his frown darkening as he tried to decipher his motives.

“I wasn’t told that,” Cynthia said slowly, one of her hands fluttering to rest against her stomach. “Though I must thank you for being with him, and for visiting. Healer Turner told me that you have been coming almost every day to sit with him.”

“Nathan is my friend,” was all Black replied with.

Simon barely heard him, still stunned by the mention of Black’s diligent bedside vigil.

Cynthia melted completely at the simple statement; at the firmness it was said with. “I am glad to hear that, Mr. Black.” An honest smile bloomed over her lips, and it was then that Simon realised what Black was angling for.

Endearment.

His fists shook. The sensation of being trapped crashed down on him, because there really was nothing that he could do to stop this without making his mother question why he did not want the other boy around. Black had played them both excellently – backing Simon into a corner, keeping him off balance and forcing his silence; then putting on this act of a concerned friend, something a blind person could tell Nathan lacked, all to try and worm his way into Cynthia’s regard.

And it had worked.

“I hope you forgive my forwardness, Lady Ciro, but may I ask something?”

“You may, but please, call me Cynthia,” his mother said, taking a step towards Black and opening her stance up. “Any friend of my children need not be so formal with me.”

“Then I insist on you calling me Orion, Cynthia,” Black seized the opportunity with greedy hands, a charming smile in place.

Simon wanted to scream, but his jaw was locked tight.

“I know this is presumptuous of me, but I was wondering if Nathan will be returning to Hogwarts? After he recovers, of course.”

He startled at the question, head swinging to stare at his mother. He did not know what answer he wanted her to give, only that he needed it with blinding urgency.

A curtain of contrition fell over Cynthia’s face, and Simon knew what she was going to say even as she opened her mouth. “I’m afraid not,” she said kindly, casting an apologetic glance at Simon. “Given everything that’s happened, we thought Nathan will benefit more from being privately tutored for a time. Perhaps in a year or two…but that is something we will decide later.”

“I see,” Black murmured, looking to the side.

Simon shook himself out of his own stupor at the idea that Nathan might never come back to Hogwarts if their mother had any say – if he could read her reluctance like a book, he knew Black could as well – and focussed on the other boy.

“In that case, would I be able to visit Nathan at your home?” Black asked, utterly ignoring the wide-eyed stare Simon levelled at him. “I was intending to write letters, but I would miss him terribly and thought, perhaps during the holidays or once he’s out of the hospital, that I might occasionally see him?”

“Your parents would not object?”

“My father has already agreed,” Black told them, and Simon mirrored his mother’s surprise.

The Blacks were notoriously closed off from most other non-Dark families, rarely mingling outside of their long-established circle. With the scandal attached to his brother, and the Ciro’s status as a relatively minor wizarding family, the fact that Arcturus Black himself would approve of his son associating with Nathan was hard to believe.

Simon looked at his mother, his heart hammering at the indecision that warred on her face.

It was perhaps one of his greatest regrets with revealing the truth around Nathan’s birth. His mother, while not a social pariah, had not escaped unscathed by the scandal. Despite there being more pity directed towards her for her husband’s infidelity than scorn for her part in the deception, Cynthia had largely retreated from many public events to maintain her dignity. It had killed a part of Simon, knowing how much his mother enjoyed socialising, to see her sacrifice that portion of her life over his actions.

That Black would dangle the possibility of a link between their families, and of their potential return to polite society, was beyond cruel.

Don’t do it, Simon wanted to cry. Don’t listen to him.

Unfortunately, his mother was not a legilimens. She accepted Black’s words at face value. “Then it would be lovely to have you,” she said, her smile returning. “Whenever you would like to visit, you may send a letter, and we can organise something.”

Black’s face lit up in boyish appreciation. “Thank you, Cynthia. I’ll leave you two alone now, but I’ll be sure to send an owl. Have a good day.”

With a low bow and bright eyes, Black walked away. He did not glance behind him, did not pause to add one last parting shot at Simon, and that deliberate disregard after such a concentrated and prolonged attack had him reeling.

Simon watched Black leave, feeling carved open and exposed, like his insides have been scooped out and thrown messily all over the floor for him to clean up.

“He’s a surprisingly pleasant boy,” his mother commented.

She completely missed the distress on Simon’s face.





OoO


Benedict waited until he heard the door close behind him before daring to release the breath he had been holding. The warmth of Cynthia’s hand still lingered on his, and he held onto that fading sensation because Merlin knew he needed the strength now.

He eyed the figure in the bed, clenching his hands rhythmically to work some of the tension out.

Nathan looked small.

His son had always been a thin little thing, but in the simple cotton garbs the hospital had dressed him in, he was diminished. The clothes draped over his frame, billowing out around his arms and making the muscles he had been building appear less than. His hair was an unbrushed mess as well – long enough now that if this were a normal day Benedict might have recommended cutting it – and there were deep black marks under his eyes that sucked the colour from his face.

His injuries were all neatly wrapped, thankfully, and with the blanket folded comfortably over his lap it was almost easy to imagine them somewhere else. Somewhere more kind, with less painful memories attached to the walls.

But reality was vicious, and it smothered the fantasy before it could live because the white bandages around Nathan’s neck were a beacon and his son was as still and static as a porcelain doll. He stared ahead with unfocussed eyes and a flat expression that had not cracked when they had entered, nor through the entire conversation with the healer, nor when Cynthia had tried to hold him.

Benedict’s throat grew tight when he thought back to his wife’s tear-stained face. At the glimmer of hurt in her eyes when Nathan had leaned away from her touch.

He looked down at his shoes, swallowing to dislodge the stone that choked him, and waited another minute to see if Nathan would acknowledge him.

Turner had assured them, in between his explanation of the calming draught and future treatment options, that Nathan was fully aware of his surroundings; but that did nothing to erase the ache in Benedict’s chest, or the shame and guilt that gnawed at him for his complete failure to protect his boy.

Your fault, every scratch and bruise that marred his son’s skin seemed to whisper.

Benedict closed his burning eyes. Please, he prayed to whatever force might listen, please.

Taking even one step forward was a herculean task, but he could not stand to remain in place a moment more. His boots squeaked on the polished floor and Nathan’s head twitched at the sound. It was a tiny tilt that became a sluggish turn, and while his eyes did not quite reach Benedict’s, it was the first sign of proper consciousness he had seen.

“Nathan?” he asked, hands curling into uncertain fists as his sides. Nathan did not respond in any manner, though he remained turned in Benedict’s direction. There was a dull glint of recognition in his eyes.

Benedict crushed the desire to surge forward and wrap his boy in his arms. The advice from Turner swirled through his mind, and the seriousness with which they had been uttered kept his limbs locked.

Don’t push him.

He inhaled sharply, fortifying himself as he continued to inch forward, giving Nathan plenty of time to object. Benedict watched for rejection, for a flinch or wince or anything that might indicate his son’s discomfort, as he rounded the end of the bed and claimed the seat against the wall. He pulled it by the arms until he could sit close beside Nathan.

Those eyes – so like his own – skittered in the air around Benedict, and it was so, so relieving because there was a time when he truly believed he would never see them again.

Settling with great care, Benedict arranged his arms in his lap and hunched his shoulders so that he was no hulking over his son. He allowed the quiet to ensnare them once more.

He did not know what to say. With Nathan, he rarely did these days.

Whatever ease that used to exist between them had been broken by his secrets, and Benedict had spent over two years trying to regain a sliver of the familial love.

My fault, he thought.

Soon, the silence grew too stifling, and confronted with this version of Nathan – blank-faced and distant in all ways but physical – he leaped on the first words that sprang forth.

“I –” he licked his lips, “I am so glad to see you, Nathan.” He pressed his hands together to stop them shaking, though his voice was still strained when he continued. “We are all so happy you’re awake.”

Nathan’s eyes flicked to his, fell away, then shifted to stare at Benedict’s right knee.

“Your mother and I will be heading home to get you some proper clothes,” he said, even though Nathan had been beside them when Turner had made the suggestion. “We’ll only be gone a short while, and your mother has asked to stay the night with you.”

Mentioning Cynthia prompted nothing, though Benedict conceded that perhaps she was too painful a topic for Nathan right now. He hurried on, “We’ve also spoken to the healer, and he said we’ll be able to take you home in the coming days. There will be plenty of time for you to recover.”

Benedict’s eyes dropped down to the bandages again. His jaw clenched, and he purposefully pulled his focus back to his son’s face. The last thing he wanted was to make Nathan uncomfortable, or self-conscious about his wound.

The scar may be large and draw attention, but Benedict would not be one of the people who will gawk at it. Gods knew it would happen enough going forward without him joining in. He refused to look at that knotted line of flesh and feel anything other than gratitude because unseemly or not, traumatic or not, that scar was proof that his boy was still alive.

Nathan did not seem to notice. Benedict blew out a breath, scrambling for something more to say.

“We’ve made the decision to pull you from Hogwarts,” he said, his words rushing out. “The school is closing early this term, but your mother and I don’t want you returning. Not – not after this. It was supposed to be a safe place,” he continued, a hint of frustration leaking through before he could squash it.

All at once, Nathan stiffened, shoulders drawn taut, and Benedict cursed himself for broaching the subject. He should not have mentioned Hogwarts or alluded to the attack. Fuck. Turner had told him that.

To soothe the hurt he had rashly caused, Benedict reached out. His hand hung in the air between them for a moment before resting atop one of Nathan’s. The skin beneath his palm was cool, so much so that he wanted to grip Nathan tight and give him some of his warmth.

“That doesn’t matter now though. We will be with you every step of the way, Nate,” he said, the old nickname slipping out softly. “We love you so much – your mother, Simon and I. We want you to get better, and we will support you however we can, in whatever way you need, while you heal.”

Grey eyes finally rose to meet his, and Benedict felt the oncoming prickle of tears – but any joy he felt withered when Nathan, with deliberate care, pulled his hand out from under his.

It hurt.

Gods, did it hurt.

He had thought that seeing Nathan in a coma would be the worst thing he could experience, that the uncertainty of his son ever opening his eyes again was the truest agony he could feel.

But it was this. This perpetual cycle they had found themselves in, with fate playing with their lives and happiness like a cat batting around a toy. It was the torture of being given a scrap of hope and then having it snatched away, again and again.

Benedict sat back in his chair, his hand coming up to press against his mouth to suppress the sob that desperately wanted to break free.

He had done this, had been the one to put Nathan here, through his cowardice and inaction. It was his fault his son was so reserved, so reduced, where before he had had a spark in him that had shone brilliantly. It was his fault that Nathan had been questioning his place in their family, and that his relationships with Cynthia and Simon were so damaged now.

It was his goddamn fault that Nathan had been attacked so many months ago on that awful Samhain night.

His handling of everything – how he told the boys, how the rumours spread, how he ignored the letters, how he had not seen Nathan crumbling before his very eyes – had been abysmal. Benedict had never been more ashamed of himself. He clamped his eyes shut, grappling for his composure even as it fell through his fingers like grains of sand.

He sat there for minutes, struggling to breathe and wishing for the ground to open, drag him down, and put him out of his misery.

The whole time, Nathan sat across from him, face half-turned away and eyes lowered. No matter if it was an act of indifference, Benedict still found himself pathetically grateful that his son would not look at him – that he gave Benedict the small mercy of not witnessing his breakdown.

It was more than he deserved.

Sitting back, Benedict rubbed his hands over his face. His gaze roamed endlessly for something to distract himself, anything other than his son’s dispassionate state, and landed on the simple brown paper bag Cynthia had left on the side table. She had been so determined to bring it, but seeing Nathan with her own eyes had distracted her, and she had dropped it without thought.

Benedict grabbed it now, the paper crinkling in his hands, the sound explosive in such a small space. He slid forward in his seat so that his knees brushed against the mattress, and this time it was he who avoided the other’s eyes. “Your mother got these for you,” he said, offering the gift. “She…she thought it might help.”

Nathan stared at the package wearily, making no move to touch it.

He forced himself to wait patiently, to not push anymore than he already had – and was rewarded when after nearly a minute Nathan’s slim hand reached out to take the bag. Benedict watched as his son opened it and pulled out the black leatherbound book. A quill fell into Nathan’s hand next, the feather a vibrant green and blue.

The boy held both items as if he had never seen their like before.

“The quill is self-inking. It’s so you can communicate with us while your throat heals,” Benedict told him quietly. “We’ll get through this, Nathan. Your voice will come back, and even if it doesn’t, we will find another way for you to talk. You are not alone, okay?”

His son swallowed thickly, chin dipping down as if to hide. He sniffed, squeezing his eyes shut and gritting his teeth. Benedict refrained from touching, though he longed to comfort his boy.

Slowly, Nathan gathered himself enough to flip the book open. The nib of the quill hovered over the paper’s surface, inching closer before lifting abruptly, over and over, until his son finally committed and scrawled something onto the page. He stared down at the words for a long moment, then turned it so Benedict could read it.

I want to go home.

“Oh, my son,” Benedict rasped, his eyes stinging anew. “My sweet boy, we’ll get you home soon, I promise. You just need to wait a little longer.”

But Nathan shook his head, expression still eerily blank despite the tears that started to spill down his cheeks in steady streams. The sight of him crying, so overwhelmed that even the fading calming draught could not hold it all back, had Benedict breaking his word and gathering his son up in his arms.

“It’ll be alright. It’s okay. You’re okay.”

Nathan tucked his head into Benedict’s neck and trembled.

“You’ll be okay, son,” Benedict murmured, his own cheeks growing wet.





OoO


Leopolda had decided, within an hour of arriving on the ship, that she rather disliked England.

There was no one thing that had prompted her swift and damning assessment of the country, nor a singular reason she could name for the irritation that had made a home in her chest. It was more a collection of minor nuisances that had piled ever higher as she had moved from the grime-covered streets choked with muggles into the slightly less congested streets of magical London.

The noises, the smells, the pervasive fear – it saturated the air and corroded her senses until she could feel the pump of it through her blood.

Perhaps the thrum of an overcrowded city would not have been so bad if she had not spent the last few months in the Black Forest, overseeing one of their remote outposts and dealing with the band of rogue poachers that were hassling them. She should have taken more time to rest between assignments, to reintegrate herself properly, then she would not be so disgusted by the dredges of society.

If Leopolda was not as dedicated to their cause, or if she loved Klaus a shade less than she did, she would have shoved this mission off on someone else.

As it was, she was now sequestered in a room of a little inn that sat in an offshoot of Knockturn Alley, its name not even memorable enough to warrant a sign. Leopolda leaned against the wall beside the window, her teeth sinking into her apple and sucking the juices before they had a chance to spurt and dribble down her arm.

She considered the darkened street below her as shadowed figures scuttled back and forth, their features hidden well by hoods and thick cloaks that made identifying even their species impossible.

Tilting forward, she watched with intrigue as a deal of some kind occurred in the alleyway beside her – a sack of galleons exchanged for a bottle that shimmered in the low light – before shaking her head and turning away. Munching on her apple, Leopolda’s eyes scanned over the interior of her accommodations, falling on the newspaper that was laid out on the small table.

Distaste dusted her expression.

She had grabbed it on her way here, interested to see what the British wizarding populace deemed newsworthy, only to be met with sharp disappointment.

It was not necessarily surprising that the Hogsmeade attack still took up a large portion of the pages. The village, and more importantly the school it was associated with, was one of the most well known and significant locations in the country. Anyone who had attended Hogwarts had likely visited Hogsmeade at some point, so everyone – regardless of if they had children enrolled currently – had a personal connection to the assault.

It did not even matter that the information given to the public was so heavily doctored. The articles did absolutely nothing to minimise how terrible the damage was. The anger and rampant calls for justice dripped off the page, each keystroke of the reporters’ typewriters another damning blow to their Lord’s credibility and support base.

Benat had really fucked them over with his choices. Whatever Klaus had done to him was not punishment enough in her opinion.

Leopolda took another, more aggressive, bite of her apple and then banished it with a tap of her wand. It no longer tasted sweet.

Wiping her hand on her pants, she walked to the table and snatched up the newspaper. She flipped through the quagmire of politicians’ quotes and inflammatory headlines until she reached the small column that had made purchasing this garbage worth it.

Student still hospitalised.

It was as buried as a story could be, though Leopolda could not guess if it was due to an editor’s disinterest or pressure from the Ciros. The boy, Nathan, was not even named, nor were his injuries outlined beyond a bland ‘severe’.

Not that it mattered. All she had wanted was the confirmation, and it saved her taking a trip to St Mungo’s and trying to get into their files.

Leopolda tossed the newspaper down, staring at the bold lettering on the back page for a moment.

The talks of Hogwarts closing early had been another lit fire to the people of Britain. It was an unprecedented move, and one met with equal parts approval and protest. Never in its history had the school cut a term short, and the whispers were growing stronger – questioning the permanency of the closure.

Losing one of the most prominent magical schools in the world…she could not help but wonder what her Lord thought of it.

Grindelwald valued magical children, she knew that personally. She and her brother had been plucked from obscurity and an early death on cold streets by the man, raised higher than they had dared to dream in their youth.

Legacy. That was what Grindelwald saw when he looked at the next generation.

Such a blatant attack by one of his own on a target everyone in their movement knew was off limits – it must have burned.

Heaving a sigh, Leopolda collapsed back on the lounge. Her head lolled to the side, looking down at the open file that Klaus had prepared for her. She tapped a finger against the photo of Nathan Ciro with his small smile and soft eyes, before her hand inched away to another.

A woman stared back at her – blonde wavy hair, bright blue eyes, a gentle expression. Beautiful in the polished way a daughter of a pureblood family often was.

Cynthia Ciro. Wife to a disloyal husband. Mother to another woman’s son. Too kind for her own good.

Leopolda found her fascinating. She could not name another woman in high society that would raise her husband’s affair child alongside her legitimate heir, and by all accounts, treat them as equals. No signs of favouritism, abuse or bitterness cracking the familial façade – from the parents at least.

Her mouth pinched at the thought of the second boy, and what Klaus had managed to dig up. As a sister, as a sibling, Simon Ciro made her skin itch unpleasantly.

She closed her eyes, breathing out roughly, and leaned her head back. Her thoughts turned back to the mother, to Cynthia, who would be her way in.

Could she use a friend? A confidante? A lover, someone who could help pay back the pain her husband’s infidelity had caused her?

Leopolda hummed, eyes slitting open to stare at the ceiling.

She would have to do more reconnaissance, because while she trusted Klaus’ efforts, she always preferred to draw her own conclusions. She would focus in on Cynthia, figure out the woman’s demeanour as much as she could before making contact. Whether that was as a friend, or something else, she would decide later.

Leopolda sat up, brushing her hair back as she stood and made her way to the desk. She had little in the way of an update, but Klaus would appreciate hearing from her anyway.

Chapter Twenty-two


Chapter Notes

Thanks for all the patience and support, guys! I've only done a brief edit of this chapter, so any spelling mistakes are absolutely a feature and not a bug 😉

Tom will make a reappearance soon, I promise!



See the end of the chapter for more notes



Her thoughts were stuck in the whirlwind, caught and thrown about like grains of sand in a storm, until she felt utterly blinded by the enormity of everything that had happened.

Cynthia took a breath - tremulous, uneven, the air sticking uncomfortably in the back of her throat - as she moved out of the gaping fireplace that dominated the lounge room's wall. Her heels sunk into the plush carpet that spanned the floor, and her hem trailed ash after her every step.

Her hands were shaking, the quivering so strong it ricocheted up her arms, so she fisted them in the skirt of her dress. The familiar pressure of tears was beating behind her eyes, leaving them aching and raw.

She was so incredibly tired.

The urge to slip into her bed, burrow under her quilts and let the exhaustion drag her to oblivion was near overwhelming, and she eyed the door leading to the grand staircase with the intensity of a hunted animal.

If she could just let her churning mind rest for an hour or two…

But there were things to do - clothes to collect, forms to fill out, preparations to make - and Nathan was still waiting for them to return.

To bring him home, like he asked.

Benedict had told her of the request, whispered it in her ear when he had joined them in the hallway, and Cynthia had almost broken down from the weight of her grief.

Lifting her hand to massage at her temple, Cynthia sighed softly and squeezed her eyes shut. She heard the fireplace roar to life once, then twice, and spoke without turning. "Ben," she said, breaking the fragile silence that had clung to them all the way from Nathan's hospital room, "can you please gather some clothes for Nathan? Make sure to include some shoes and socks for him, and toiletries. He will likely want a shower before getting dressed."

Her fingers crawled down her face to hover over her mouth. She kept the touch delicate; deliberately light to remind herself not to claw her own flesh, because no wound she inflicted on herself would make up for even a fraction of what Nathan had suffered.

"His things should be in his trunk. Professor Dumbledore returned it yesterday."

It had been a fraught conversation with her old transfiguration professor. The man had never looked as worn as he had standing on their doorstep, Nathan's suitcase clasped in his gloved hands.

He had apologised for what had happened, and Cynthia did not quite remember what she had said in response. It had been accusatory though - the words spitted out with refined callousness - because all she had been able to think of in that moment were the whispers that surrounded Dumbledore.

Of his supposed history with Gellert Grindelwald. Of their matching power levels. Of the meetings with the I.W.C and their requests for Dumbledore to deal with things.

Of his refusal.

But this was no longer a distant issue in mainland Europe. This was no longer something Britain could afford to ignore.

People had died on their own soil. Students, children, had been killed. Her son was in the hospital after fighting for his life.

And Dumbledore had done nothing.

Cynthia remembered little beyond snatching her son's trunk from the man's hands and slamming the door in his face.

"I will go to the office and get the forms filled in," she continued, blinking rapidly to clear her hazy vision. "If we're quick enough, we can get them filled and have Nathan here before tonight. He will need time to adjust, but with the meal and treatment plans we can fix this."

She could fix this. She would fix this.

"Tomorrow I can start looking for a tutor, someone to help him on top of his studies. They need to specialise in defence."

So this never happens again, she thought, anger and sorrow gnawing at her bones. She would find someone who could teach her son to defend himself so well that no one would ever be able to hurt him again.

She did not care who or what; so long as Nathan never had to see the inside of Hogwarts or St Mungo's again.

"Giffy can prepa -"

"Cynthia."

Her words cut off, and she turned with a sharp frown. Benedict sounded strange, his voice a rough entreaty, and when she looked she found him staring at Simon with a helpless expression.

Cynthia's eyes shot to their son, and she felt her heart break anew.

Simon stood apart from them, his face averted, but the shaking of his shoulders betrayed him.

"Oh, my sweet boy," she whispered, her agitation dying a swift death. Concern rose in its place, and Cynthia swept forward to pull him to her chest, one hand cradling the back of his head.

She shushed him, her lips brushing against his temple, feather-light but burning with love. "Hush, darling, it's alright. It's alright."

Simon continued to shake and though his arms remained firmly curled around himself, he still buried his face into her shoulder, hiding from the world like he had as a child.

Cynthia's eyes stung, a fresh well of tears springing forth as was always the case when she saw one of her boys hurting.

Thick arms wrapped around them both, and a broad, warm chest pressed against her side. Benedict's touch was both welcome and unwelcome - and Cynthia was torn by the desire to shove him away just as much as she wanted to push herself closer.

She simply tucked her face into Simon's hair and breathed through the bitterness.

They stayed like that for a few long minutes, Cynthia's hand gently soothing over her son's head until a deep, shuddering sigh rattled through Simon.

"What am I supposed to do?" he croaked, the words muffled but still audible.

Cynthia's frown pulled tighter. "What do you mean, sweetheart?" she asked, leaning back just enough to try and catch his eyes.

Simon avoided her attempt, staring down at his shoes with his shoulders hunched. He was chewing on his bottom lip, clearly at war with himself, before finally blurting out, "What's going to happen to me?"

Cynthia shook her head lightly, not understanding. Benedict traded a confused glance with her above his head, before clarity struck Cynthia like a slap to the face.

"Oh, Simon," she said softly, cupping his face in her hands, "if this is about us pulling Nathan from school, you do not have to worry. You can stay enrolled if you wish, or we can get you your own tutor, separate from Nathan's so you both get the attention you need. We have options. We just want to get Nathan sorted and then -"

Simon pushed away from her, strong enough that Cynthia would have been knocked back had Benedict not been behind her. She stared in bewilderment as Simon rubbed at his eyes, fruitlessly trying to wipe away the tears that were spilling down his cheeks.

"I'm not talking about Nathan!" he snapped, digging the heels of his palms into his eyes. His words were warbling, cracking under the strain and volume he spoke with. "I'm talking about me! Why does - why does everything always have to be about him?"

Cynthia's hands dropped to her stomach, grasping each other and twisting her fingers together. She could only watch as her son continued to cry.

"I can't - it's not - why can't you just -" Simon hiccupped, shying away from them further and almost knocking into the low table between the lounges.

"Why can't you just look at me for one minute?"

His hands fell to his sides and he stared at her, not them, her, with blood-shot, watery eyes.

Cynthia's voice withered in her throat.

What could she even say after such an accusation? What could possibly make right the sins she had so clearly committed if her son was driven to tears over it?

How much had she failed as a mother that she had not even noticed?

Cynthia could admit that their focus had been on Nathan recently, that Simon had often fallen to the side in a lot of their decisions and conversations. But with everything that had happened, with everything Nathan had gone through, it just felt so hard to divide their attention these days.

There were so many moving parts to keep track of, too many directions they were being pulled in - and Simon had always been so steady.

Collected, unbothered, unobtrusive Simon. Her perfect, easy son.

"Simon -"

"Why…why did you keep him?" Simon asked then, his voice miserable and so small. His eyes were clamped shut, his mouth trembling, and he looked braced to take a hit.

Cynthia's breath hitched, the only sound in the sudden quiet that descended on the room. Her fingers found her wedding band, twisting it around.

"Son," Benedict said, his eyes wide and distraught, but Simon sent him a glare so cutting it stalled whatever else her husband wanted to say.

"I'm not asking you," he spat, fists clenching at his sides. "I know why you did it. I want to know why you went along with it." Simon's gaze flew back to her, desperate and beseeching and looking years younger than he was.

"We…" she began, licking her lips, "we already discussed this."

"No, we didn't," Simon argued, shaking his head hard. "You talked at us, and then we never spoke of it again."

They had.

Cynthia twisted her ring again and again. Her heart wished to forget that awful, painful day, but she knew her mind never would.

She could never forget the tragic, shadowed look that had stolen across Simon's face, nor the way Nathan - bright, kind Nathan - had shut down before her eyes.

The weight of Benedict's lie might have been crushing them all, slowly leeching the love and warmth out of their marriage, even if the boys had not known why. It had needed to be revealed, it had not been fair to the children to keep it from them, for making them live a lie - but a part of her would always regret telling them the truth.

She had lost something precious that day.

From the corner of her eye, Cynthia saw Benedict shift. Her husband went to take a step forward, his arm raised and mouth opened, but he halted when her hand snapped out to encircle his wrist.

"Leave us," she said, quiet and firm.

Benedict looked at her sharply, a protest on the tip of his tongue, though it died when she met and held his gaze.

"Ben. Leave."

The fight drained from him, and the defeat on his face was too similar to the night he had shown up, bundle in his arms and guilt clinging to his clothes like a physical stench.

He backed away a step, and she released his wrist before turning back to Simon. She did not need to watch him leave. There would be another argument in their future, she knew, but for now her focus was on her son.

The door clicked shut behind Benedict, and despite his earlier words demanding just this, Simon's face was pale and he was shivering. He looked more haunted than relieved, more scared than satisfied.

Cynthia quietly tallied another sin against herself.

No child should ever fear their mother.

"Come, darling," she beckoned, holding out a hand, "let's sit."

Simon wavered, caught between his wants, but he eventually shuffled over to her. She guided him over to the closest lounge, sitting down carefully and tugging his hand until he joined her. He perched himself awkwardly on the edge, ready to fly away at a moment's notice.

Cynthia folded her hands together on her lap, took a deep breath, and began to speak.

"When your father first brought Nathan here, I had not seen him in over a month. I had just given birth to you, alone, because of his conflicting business trip. I had no idea of the affair until that moment, and I was very close to demanding a divorce."

Simon's head snapped up to her, eyes wide and horrified. Cynthia nodded, guessing at his thoughts. People in their social circle did not take well to divorces, often looking down on both parties of a dissolved marriage, though Cynthia suspected she would have received far more criticism from outside observers.

An affair and a bastard child were not uncommon in their society, especially amongst purebloods. A man might be praised for his virility, but his wife was expected to forgive such a transgression to keep up with perception.

Cynthia, in contemplating divorce, would have been opening herself up to becoming a laughingstock.

She met Simon's gaze, keeping her expression calm and waited as realisation slowly filled his eyes. She needed to make sure he understood how seriously she had taken Benedict's betrayal. What she had been prepared to do.

"I was angry at your father. Viciously so. I refused to let him explain; refused to even look at him. My heart was shattered, and my marriage was in tatters. I was alone with a newborn and had another woman's child somewhere in my house." Her eyes fluttered shut in remembered agony. "I was so upset, not just because of the affair, but because she had given birth before me. I was hurt that Benedict was there for Nathan's birth but not yours."

She still was.

"I shut myself away for days. I kept you from him. Said many cruel and unpleasant things - both about him and Nathan." Cynthia looked down with shame. Her anger at Benedict was justified, she knew that, but she would never forgive herself for the things she said about Nathan in those early weeks.

For the things she had wished.

"Eventually, I confronted him and demanded to know if he ever would have told me about his affair if Mila hadn't died."

Beside her, Simon went rigid. Cynthia did not look at him.

It was the first time either of them had said her name.

"Your father admitted that he wouldn't have. That he had been planning to just pay her to make her go away. Nathan would have been raised in Germany. He would have gone to Durmstrang or one of the smaller academies. We never would have known."

Around and around her wedding band spun. Her eyes were distant.

"I went back to ignoring him, spent the week tending to you while he helped Nathan, who still hadn't been named. I was so furious. That he had planned to lie to me. That he had only had to admit to his mistake when his plan fell through. That he hadn't just…dealt with the problem."

The words were poison on her tongue. She thought of Nathan, of the sweet and earnest boy he had grown into, and a part of her wanted to die.

"And then one night," she continued, voice dropping low, "after I had settled you and was going to prepare some tea, I walked past your father's solar and heard crying."

Cynthia could almost hear it now.

She remembered coming to a stop in the hallway.

She remembered waiting, breathless, to hear Benedict approach and tend to his son.

And she remembered the seconds stretching into minutes. The little cries growing stronger, more distressed.

"Your father was not there. I don't remember where he went, but he had left Nathan alone. No one was around, and so I…went inside."

Had crept in like a thief, brimming with need to justify her rage.

"He was in the cot," Cynthia whispered, her voice breaking, "and he was just a baby, Simon. Just this small little thing, swaddled too tightly because your father didn't know the first thing about caring for a newborn. He was crying hard enough I thought he might choke. I just stood there, staring down at this baby that I had been blaming for simply existing - and I hated myself."

Cynthia put her face in her hands. Even now, the guilt of her own dark thoughts those first few weeks haunted her.

"He was…he was just a baby. He hadn't asked to be born. He hadn't asked to be your father's, nor Mila's, son. He had no idea of anything - just an innocent life that was brought into the world in a cruel way. And I couldn't hold onto that anger anymore. I couldn't keep blaming a child for my husband's mistake. So, I picked him up."

Cynthia turned to Simon and her eyes shone. "I picked him up and I took him back to my room. And when I laid him in that cot next to you, he settled instantly. Just…fell right asleep, as if he felt safe for the first time in his life. It was…it was magical."

She grabbed his hands in hers, squeezing tight. "You turned towards him, and seeing the two of you together, as if it was always meant to be…" she swallowed, blinking away her tears. "I told Benedict that night that I would raise him alongside you. That we would pass you off as twins. We would say it was a dangerous pregnancy and that was why we hadn't announced anything yet; and when you were old enough, we would tell you the truth."

Simon stared at her, horror on his face.

"Nathan, he - he's as much my son as you are, darling. He's your brother, not because of my adoption, or your father's blood, but because you've scarcely known a day without him, and from that night the two of you always chose each other. And I am so glad I chose him too because every day I see you together I'm reminded of how much love you brought each other."

Cynthia reached out and brushed her fingers over his cheek, and that single touch seemed to be a step too far.

Simon broke.





OoO


His face still stung from the tears, his skin now too tight, too itchy, and the exhaustion he felt went far beyond the emotional; but Harry did not let the exhaustion stop him from throwing the blanket off and carefully shifting to the edge of the bed.

He swung his legs down, scooted until his feet touched the cold floor, and pushed himself up with a silent groan.

Immediately, he swayed, almost collapsing right back into the bed with how weak his body was. Harry managed to grab onto the bedside table to steady himself, and once the tremors died down, he made his way towards the bathroom with one shuffling step at a time.

He had no time to waste. The Ciros had left half an hour ago, thankfully taking all of their uncomfortable expectations with them, and Harry could feel the calming draught slowly working its way out of his system.

He needed to be gone before then.

Harry clipped the doorway of the bathroom when he reached it, sending a jolt of pain through his whole body. His hand smacked blindly against the wall until he found the patch of runes that brightened the room, and he stumbled forward so he could slump against the porcelain sink. His arms were barely able to hold up his own weight, but he locked his elbows and forced himself upright.

Tired eyes stared back at him from the mirror. Deep bruises sat underneath them, leaching what little colour he had right from his cheeks and leaving him looking like a ghoul.

Harry studied himself critically - taking in the red rims of his eyes, the flat, greasy hair, and finally the stark white bandages that covered the entirety of his neck.

He stared at those the longest, fingers flexing around the edge of the sink.

Leaning forward, he braced himself against the porcelain with his hips and brought his hands up to the pinned edge of the first layer of bandages. Slowly, so slowly he barely realised he was even moving, Harry unhooked the clip that kept the tail of the bandage down and began to unwind it.

Round and round he went, white fabric pooling into the sink as he twisted it free, and within a minute his skin was bared.

A thick piece of gauze had been pressed to the front of his throat, and it stayed there for a brief moment, suspended by the lingering pressure of bandages, before gravity took hold. Harry watched with dark eyes as the hefty cotton pad peeled away and fell into the bundle of bandages, and then all thoughts fled his mind.

Harry released a whistling sigh.

The scar was prominent, just like Turner had said, but even with that warning…

Harry's fingers hovered just above the large rope of knotted scar tissue that wrapped nearly completely around the front of his throat. Branches splintered off from it, some inching up his jawline, others creeping down towards his collarbones - all of them shining pink with a fresh layer of skin.

His hands dropped down to the sink again, knuckles whitening from the force of his grip.

He could feel it pressing against his oesophagus, a constant weight he could not ignore. It was as if someone was holding their hand to his throat and squeezing.

It would be the first thing anyone noticed when they looked at him.

Harry bowed his head, moving with care so as not to agitate the wound.

His eyes slipped shut.

I'm sorry, he thought at the ghost whose body he had stolen. I'm sorry. I'm so sorry. You didn't deserve this.

Nathan had not deserved a lot of things that happened to him.

He had not deserved his brother's betrayal, nor the relentless bullying of his peers.

He had not deserved to be attacked that night, nor to lose his life afterwards.

He had not deserved to be drawn into Death's games, nor turned into a meat-suit for Harry to wear.

He had not deserved any of it.

I'm sorry, he thought, shoulders hunching from the shame.

But there was no one there to hear his laments.

Nathan was gone, consumed by Death months ago. All that remained now was Harry.

He opened his eyes and stared into his reflection. Blank-faced and dry-eyed, he tipped forward until his forehead rested on the cool surface of the glass and debated on how he should hide the scar going forward.

Everyone would know about it, everyone would look for it, especially when they realised he had left. Their eyes would roam the neck of every young boy they came across, hunting for a hint of the mark that would identify him. Even if he went far away, vanished across country lines and buried himself in a tiny village in the middle of nowhere, people would always see the scar first.

Harry had lived with that kind of scrutiny before, and had long grown accustomed to being seen as an extension of his scars rather than the other way around. He had learned eventually to wear them like a badge of pride, as symbols of his survival - but this time felt different.

Perhaps it was because he had not survived. Not really. He had gotten sloppy, become distracted, and had been killed for it.

The reason - the only reason - he was still here was because Death did not want him.

Harry's next breath fogged the mirror.

There was…there was nothing left for him.

No home to return to. No future he could grasp. No end waiting for him.

He would be forever. A broken remnant of a world long gone, trapped in a cycle that would always renew.

Unwanted. The useless limb of an entity so far beyond his comprehension that it terrified him beneath the haze of potion.

Should he even bother trying to disappear? What did it matter if he exploded here? What did any of this matter?

Nothing he did would have an impact if, in a trillion years, it would all just roll over and begin again.

What was the point?

Harry hunched over further, staring down at the drain with blurred vision. All around him, things began to rattle. The bottles along the shelves. The metal bar of the shower curtain. The tiles on the walls.

He had no idea how to get out of here. He had no clothes, no insight of the layout of this St Mungo's, and no clue where he could go.

Why should he even care?

His hand drifted down to his waist, pulling at the elastic of his pants, pushing it down until he could see the brand low on his hip bone.

He wanted to tear it off.

"Little early for a show, wouldn't you say?"

Harry gasped silently, and the world around him stilled. The sudden lack of noise was loud, and it took him a moment to find Orion's reflection in the mirror, leaning against the bathroom door casually.

The other boy's eyes were fixed on him, unerringly meeting his through the mirror, and though the smile on his face was amused, everything about him radiated a tension that made Harry's teeth ache in response.

Seeing that he had his attention, Orion's gaze slipped down to the exposed skin of his hip, and he raised an eyebrow. "Why Nathan," the boy said, his voice a smooth drawl as he put a scandalised hand to his chest, "you should know I'm an engaged man. Although," Orion's smile sharpened, "I could be persuaded."

Harry let go and his pants snapped back into place. He turned slowly, his lower back pressed against the sink, and blinked.

The pause was not allowed to stretch as Orion spoke once more. "Here," he said, tossing something at him. Harry caught it automatically, staring down at the familiar notebook Benedict had left for him. The self-inking quill was tucked into the cover securely.

"If you're done admiring yourself in the mirror, we've got things to do."

What?

Harry looked up, bewildered.

"Chop chop," Orion said, clapping his hands. "I've got questions and you, my dear miracle, have the answers."

Harry put the book to the side, but Orion immediately stepped close and plucked it back up. He smacked it against Harry's chest, holding it there until he took it once more.

"Nuh uh," the younger boy said, "I know you were in the middle of a breakdown but that can wait. We've more important things to discuss. Come on."

A steely hand latched onto his arm and Harry was whisked back into the main room.

"So glad I thought to sneak back in here," Orion was saying, pushing Harry back to the bed. "Your healer and his team are like a pack of gargoyles, and don't even get me started on your family."

A sneer graced the other's face, there and gone in a moment, and Harry was quietly trying to understand what was happening.

He stared up at Orion with wide eyes, and some distant, chained part of his heart could only see Sirius.

"Now, are you aware enough to have a conversation, or do I have to postpone again until after your freak-out?"

Orion watched him back, expectant and impatient as his eyes darted pointedly down to the book in Harry's lap repeatedly.

Not once did his attention drift to the scar.

Harry's hand shook.

He had…he had almost…

Orion stood before him, hands on his hips and eyebrows raised high. He was here. He was here, and he was real, and Harry had almost -

"Well?" Orion prompted, foot tapping. An errant curl fell into his face and he blew it back, just like Sirius used to, and Harry's chest hurt.

The quill was in his hand before he remembered grabbing it, and he flipped blindly to a new page. He scribbled his message quickly, his heart beginning to pound, and turned it to show the other.

I need to get out of here.

An intrigued smile overtook Orion's face, and he reached down to pick up a bag that had been left beside the bed. He tossed it next to him, a shirt and other clothes spilling out.

"Get dressed then."





OoO


Orion was quiet as Nathan changed out his hospital robes and into the plain turtleneck and trousers that he had brought on a whim.

He was quiet as they slipped out of the room and down the hall.

He was quiet as they avoided the healers on their rounds and made their way to one of the secondary entrances.

He was quiet as they stepped out into the fresh air and Nathan offered a hand.

The decision to come back to the hospital again had been more a whim than anything, brought on by the empty halls of his home and lack of letters from Gus. He could not even say what had made him bring a change of clothes with him, beyond knowing firsthand how uncomfortable the hospital's gowns were.

It had been an impulse, driven by something deep inside him, but he could not deny the results.

Nathan looked good standing like this, wearing Orion's clothes that fit him like a glove and with his hair hastily cleaned with a simple charm. It whipped around his face right now, falling messily in every direction, and when the afternoon sun caught the brown strands he appeared to glow gold.

Orion had always liked gold.

He reached out and clasped Nathan's hand, only belatedly realising what was happening when the familiar crushing sensation in his navel hit him.

They landed in a forest, and Orion only stumbled a little over the uneven ground suddenly beneath his feet. Nathan's hand squeezed his, helping him regain his balance before slipping away.

He straightened, fixing his coat as he did so, and cast his eyes about.

He could feel the lack of life around them - no birds, no insects, not even the rustle of the wind - and it was strange to go from a bustling city filled with noise to a place so far removed from everything.

They were beneath a dark canopy, surrounded by trees with trunks so thick five men would struggle to wrap their arms around them. Orion tipped his head back, eyes trailing up until he saw the blanket of leaves above them that blotted out even a hint of the sky.

He scuffed one of his boots on the dirt, sniffing quietly, and tucked his hands into his coat pockets. There was enough ambient magic in the air that he felt comfortable casting a quick warming charm, but it did little to chase away the memory of the air's chill.

Satisfied that they were alone, he brought his attention back to Nathan.

The other boy stood beside him, face still stuck in that eerie, artificial calm as he scanned the area. There was a fractured kind of recognition in his eyes, as if he had been here before but it was somehow not what he remembered, and Orion cocked his head to the side.

He wanted to ask how Nathan knew how to apparate - how he knew side-along apparation - when his class were not due to learn that for another few years yet, but there were more pressing concerns at the moment. Instead, he filed those questions away to revisit at another day, like he had countless times already.

"Why are we here?" he asked, voice cracking like a whip in the natural silence of the forest. Orion's eyes remained fixed on Nathan, though he shifted his weight.

Nathan did not respond to him right away, though he was jolted from his reverie by Orion's question. The other boy looked at him as if just remembering his presence. Ordinarily, Orion would be offended, but in the low light of the forest Nathan shone like a beacon and his hooded eyes were fathomless and heavy.

Filled with impossible things, Orion mused. He remembered black wisps and a body jerking back to life, and marvelled at this boy in front of him.

Miracle, indeed.

Nathan pulled his notebook out in a daze, writing something on the white paper before tucking the quill away and offering it to him. Orion reached out to take it, though he held it limply for a moment, just glancing between it and Nathan.

The other boy nodded at him, and began to stumble away. He only made it a few metres before he stopped and sank down to his knees in the damp dirt. His back bowed, his hands clamping into the earth to ground himself, and then he just sat there.

Orion waited, expectant, but returned his focus to the notebook when Nathan did nothing else.

He read through the hasty script once, twice, then a third time. His mouth parted as realisation hit him like a bludger to the head.

"Shit," he whispered, dropping the book like it burned and began rummaging through his pockets. "Shit, shit, shit, you stupid fucking moron, why didn't you say something before?"

Orion took off at a run, trying to get as much distance as he could as he pulled out the necklace his father had given him after Lucretia died.

He had never worn it before - had never needed to wear it before, though he rarely went anywhere without it - and Orion prayed to whatever might be listening that his father had not been exaggerating when he described how the enchantments worked.

If he had known, he never would have insisted on coming with Nathan before they had left the hospital room - but this was what his bloody curiosity got him.

He slipped the cool silver chain over his neck and sliced his thumb open along the crest of the Black family that hung from it. Instantly, a hum sounded, and a faint shimmering light wrapped around him.

Orion dropped behind the closest tree.

He tucked himself in tight, closed his eyes, and braced himself.

All he heard was one sob before the explosion of magic tore through the area.
